Stopping it early is a cluster administrator's `kubectl delete agentsandbox`, which the finalizer turns into a release; there is no broker route for an administrator to delete another person's sandbox | Bounded by the claim's TTL and the claim's own audiences; the same administrator and Kubernetes audit log that already govern the declarative path are the break-glass (`a_caller_who_left_the_group_can_no_longer_read_or_delete_their_claim` shows the refusal and that the claim is still there); a narrower rule (a namespace administrator deleting in their own namespace) was rejected as a second authority over a claim | An admin delete route exists, or an operator needs revocation at the IdP to end sandboxes without cluster access |
| R-36 | *New 2026-11-17 (ADR-0046).* For a sandbox outside the broker's own namespace the session and delegation Secrets (in the broker's namespace) carry no `ownerReference`, since one cannot cross a namespace, so Kubernetes garbage collection never removes them: only the release and the 15-second sweep do. A Secret that outlives its sandbox because both paths failed is sealed under the sandbox's UID and bounded by its envelope expiry, as R-33 already accepts | `owner_refs` returns `None` outside the home namespace (`a_session_is_owned_by_its_sandbox_only_within_the_brokers_namespace`, `crates/mediatore-claims/src/kubestore.rs`); `revoke_claim` deletes every session Secret labelled with the sandbox UID through `delete_sessions_of`, and `sweep` every Secret so labelled through `delete_secrets_of` (`releasing_a_claim_in_a_tenant_namespace_deletes_its_session_and_delegation_secrets`, `the_sweep_removes_a_tenant_sandboxs_secrets_when_the_release_was_missed`); the expiry sweep of TB-9 bounds the rest | A shipped path creates a sandbox in another namespace without the release or the sweep running (or the ciphertext-retention reading of R-33 changes) *Corrected 2026-11-19:* until that date the issuer's own session Secret carried no expiry, so "bounded by its envelope expiry" held for delegations only; it carries one now, and the sweep also removes a claimed Secret whose sandbox is gone (TB-9) *Corrected again 2026-11-19 (ADR-0057):* this row cited `delete_secrets_of` for the release, and until that change a release did not delete Secrets through it. A release now deletes the session Secrets on every call, through `delete_sessions_of` (the stored output's segments are not sessions and stay for their retention), and `delete_secrets_of` is the sweep's |
| R-37 | *New 2026-11-17 (ADR-0046).* `allow_any_authenticated: true` on an issuer is an operator choice that admits anyone the IdP authenticates to log in to the broker (`me` and the refresh path included). With a namespace map such a login can claim nowhere unless a namespace lists a group it holds; without a map it can claim in the one open namespace | The marker must be written (an issuer with no gate refuses to start, `Config::check_gates`, `an_issuer_with_no_gate_key_refuses_to_start`, `a_google_issuer_needs_the_explicit_marker`) and shows in the startup line as `none (explicit)` (`startup_logs_the_gate_form_per_issuer`); the Google case, where who may log in is decided at Google, is the guide's example; with a map the namespace groups are the only gate to a claim | An issuer carrying `allow_any_authenticated` also serves a population that should not log in at all, or a second IdP is added behind the same marker |
| R-38 | *New 2026-11-17 (ADR-0045).* Redaction of the workload's output is best effort, not a guarantee: the guest removes the secrets it knows it placed in the jail and what has the shape of a credential, and cannot catch a secret the workload transforms (split, hex, reversed, encrypted), an opaque token the egress proxy fetched (it lives in the proxy's process and is matched by shape only), a credential the workload derived or fetched itself from a destination the policy allows, or personal and proprietary data that is not a credential. What bounds the exposure is not the redactor: the output is readable only by the claim's subject, who is already entitled to the claim's credentials, and the broker's Secrets, already the broker's trust domain (ADR-0034), hold it for `logs.max_bytes` and `logs.retention_secs` at most. A requester whose task involves confidential material leaves the opt-in off, and the default is off. *Amended 2026-11-20:* a line that has not ended is now shipped in pieces, and a bare carriage return ends a line; neither moves this limit, since each piece is cut by the redactor with its end held back until the next bytes are read (TB-6), so a known secret or a shaped one written across a pause is still seen whole, and a secret split by a carriage return is one split across lines, already above. *Revisit when* the output becomes readable by anyone but the owner (an admin route, a declarative reader, roadmap 13), or when a backend issues credentials the shape rules do not match | Accepted |
| R-39 | *New 2026-11-17 (ADR-0045); narrowed 2026-11-19 (ADR-0052).* The output is sealed at rest, so a cluster administrator, or anything with `get` on Secrets in the broker's namespace, or a copy of etcd, no longer reads a claim's output. What still does: anything that can act as the broker at OpenBao, namely its ServiceAccount token (audience `openbao`), a shell in its pod, or the operator of OpenBao itself, because the transit context is the claim UID and a label, neither secret, and OpenBao does not restrict decryption per claim; and the plaintext of a batch in the broker's memory for the length of a call. This is the same trust point as the sealed sessions beside the output (R-4); it is break-glass, visible in the audit logs of the API server and of OpenBao, and not a mediatore feature; no API override for operators or admins exists. The earlier reasons for not sealing (a replica must serve it without a key round trip, sealing changes nothing through the API) were weighed in ADR-0052 and do not hold: every replica already reaches the key and the point was the reader without the API. *Revisit when* the deployment wants output unreadable even to the broker after the claim ends (a key per claim the broker deletes at release) | Accepted |
| R-40 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0052).* Sealing makes OpenBao a participant in the output path: it sees each batch's plaintext as it encrypts (so an OpenBao audit device configured to log raw request bodies would write output into its log, a setting mediatore neither controls nor can detect), and every read of a ring costs it one decrypt per stored blob (at most `logs.max_bytes` of weight over 256 bytes of overhead per blob, so about 4000 blobs at the 1 MiB default and 16000 at the ceiling, four at a time, newest first for a tail, none behind a cursor; a follower costs only what is new). The read is owner-only, so the amplification is the owner's own, and an OpenBao outage is a `503`, not a disclosure. The batch is also sealed again on each of the three attempts an optimistic write conflict between replicas can cost. *Amended 2026-11-20:* a claim that keeps printing now costs up to two `encrypt` calls a second where it cost one about every two seconds (the batch limit, TB-6), in smaller batches, and a follower of it reads, and so decrypts what is new, up to as often; the blobs per ring are bounded by weight as before. *Revisit when* an OpenBao operator will not accept plaintext of workload output in its trust (then seal in the broker with a data key wrapped by transit, one call per claim), or when read load on OpenBao is the complaint (then larger blobs, one per segment rewrite) | Accepted |
| R-41 | *New 2026-11-19 (adversarial review).* The build and release pipeline is part of what a deployment trusts (A-17) and has no boundary of its own in this document: a dependency, action or base-image update that passes the end-to-end gate after its cooldown can merge without a person reading it (`.github/dependabot.yml`, `.github/workflows/dependabot-auto-merge.yaml`), the merge is built, signed and attested as `main`, and the `latest` tag moves with it (`.github/workflows/build.yaml`). A signature and an attestation prove which workflow built an artifact, not that what it was built from was read | Pre-1.0 with one maintainer: releases are tagged by hand, the release manifest and both `VMImage`s are pinned by digest and checked (`scripts/check_release_assets.py`), cargo sources are allow-listed and advisories denied (`deny.toml`), every action is pinned by commit. An operator who deploys a digest from a release and verifies its attestation against the Build workflow and the release tag is not following `latest` | A second maintainer or a first deployment outside the maintainer's own; then the pipeline gets a boundary and STRIDE rows of its own, and the update automation is narrowed |
| R-42 | *New 2026-11-19 (adversarial review); rewritten the same day, ADR-0051 having merged.* The seccomp filter (ADR-0051, `crates/mediatore-guest/src/seccomp.rs`) closes the socket families the `inet` table and Landlock cannot name, `AF_VSOCK` to the hypervisor's host among them, and refuses `io_uring`, `bpf`, `unshare` and `setns`. *Narrowed 2026-11-19:* the filter now reads a socket's protocol argument, so an inet stream socket of a protocol other than TCP (MPTCP) is refused by the filter (`INET_STREAM_PROTOCOLS`, TB-12) as well as dropped by the uid-keyed table and turned off in the image by `net.mptcp.enabled = 0`. One thing it leaves: it does not refuse `clone` or `clone3` with `CLONE_NEWUSER`, so a child user namespace, and the kernel surface behind one, is still the workload's to create. The sandbox image's sysctl layer (`image/sysctl/99-mediatore.conf`) sets Yama's scope and, since 2026-11-19, turns off `io_uring`, MPTCP and unprivileged BPF and hides kernel pointers and the kernel log, asserted by `image/check.sh` | A filter on `clone`'s flags cannot see `clone3`'s, which are behind a pointer, so the bound for user namespaces belongs in the image (`user.max_user_namespaces = 0`), and that needs a boot showing `nsjail` and the SPIRE agent still start; meanwhile `nsjail` by its defaults drops every capability and sets `no_new_privs`, and the jail passes no flag that turns either off (`JailSpec::to_args`, `crates/mediatore-guest/src/jail.rs`; a default, not a test of this repository), Landlock handles the filesystem and TCP (`confine.rs`), a uid-keyed table drops an MPTCP socket as it drops TCP (tested on the image's kernel line, 2026-11-19), the VM is the outer bound (ADR-0010) and it is destroyed with the claim | The image sets `user.max_user_namespaces = 0` after a live boot proves it (the other half of this condition, the filter requiring protocol 0 or TCP for an inet stream, was met 2026-11-19); then delete this row |
| R-43 | *New 2026-11-19 (adversarial review); narrowed the same day.* Both VM images carry `qemu-guest-agent` (`image/Dockerfile`, `image/gateway/Dockerfile`), and since 2026-11-19 an allow-list for it, `/etc/qemu/qemu-ga.conf` (`image/qemu-ga.conf`, `image/gateway/qemu-ga.conf`): the eight commands banlieue's libvirt provider sends and no other, so no exec, no file write, no password and no key injection, asserted in both built images and confirmed as read by the agent itself (`image/check.sh`, `image/gateway/check.sh`). What is left: the list is by command and not by argument, so whoever holds the hypervisor's end of that channel, root on the host or the provider credential banlieue uses, can still read any file root can read in a pool member and in a gateway, and can create or truncate a file by opening it in a writing mode. "No listener on the segment" (TB-4) does not bound a channel that is not on the segment | The hypervisor and banlieue's provider credentials are already trusted for the VM (R-6, banlieue's own threat model): a host that can run a guest can also read its memory. The agent is there because the libvirt provider reads the guest's addresses and the installed signal through it (ADR-0019) | The allow-list is run against a real libvirt provider on a booted member (not yet done: a missing command would show as a member that never reports `GuestReady`); and the provider reads its three files by something narrower than a general file read, or the agent can restrict paths |
| R-44 | *New 2026-11-19 (adversarial review).* Rights on a pool are rights on what its members are handed. The watcher declares a node entry for any `VirtualMachine` that carries the pool label and publishes an endorsement certificate its host's CA signed (`reconcile_machine`, `crates/mediatore-claims/src/watch.rs`), in any namespace, and the audiences and the registry credential are the broker's configuration, not a namespace's: a person who may choose a pool's image in one namespace is root on its members, and a sandbox bound there is handed what any sandbox is, an operator-held credential that is not gateway-placed (A-23) and the pull credential (A-16) included. The namespace map (A-26) governs who may claim, not who may run a pool | In the deployments this was built for the operator alone creates pools and images (ADR-0029), and TB-2 already records that a pool's editor decides what its members run; gateway placement (ADR-0040) keeps a credential off every pool VM whatever image it runs | A second team is given rights on pools or images, or before a multi-team deployment: then the operator lists the pools (or image digests) whose members may become nodes, and shared credentials are gateway-placed or scoped to a namespace |
| R-45 | *New 2026-11-19 (adversarial review).* The base deployment puts the broker in banlieue's namespace and grants it every verb on every Secret there (`deploy/base/kustomization.yaml`, the `mediatore-sessions` Role in `deploy/base/rbac.yaml`), since a Role cannot name Secrets that do not exist yet. Two things follow that R-4 does not say: a compromised broker reads and rewrites whatever else is kept in that namespace, and anything granted `get` on Secrets there for banlieue's sake reads the STS signing key and, with the local sealer, the sealing key beside the envelopes it opens | One namespace was the shape the first deployment had; the transit sealer (ADR-0015) keeps the sealing key out of the namespace altogether, and the cluster's administrators are trusted (§9) | The broker moves to a namespace of its own (the two admission policies and the `ClusterSPIFFEID` name the namespace and move with it), or a second principal is granted Secret access in the shared one |
| R-46 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0057; its decision 8, a MAC over the record, is deferred and not built).* A claim's record is believed because it is on a status and names its own sandbox (`binding_of`, `crates/mediatore-claims/src/kubestore.rs`); it carries no signature. A principal who can write `agentsandboxes/status` and is not the broker, on a cluster where the status admission policy is absent, unbound or does not list a served version, can write a record with the right `sandboxUid` and the broker reads it as its own | The admission policy admits the broker's ServiceAccount alone and closed the case that was found (`deploy/base/admission/agentsandbox-status.yaml`, TB-2). The writers it does not stop (one who can change or unbind it, one who can act as the broker, a writer of the datastore) can reach the sealer as the broker does, so they could make whatever tag the broker makes. A forged record's pointers open nothing: every envelope is bound to its sandbox and its slot (TB-9). A MAC would put a sealer call on the first read of every record and give an outage or a key rotation a way to end live claims | A deployment must run without the admission policy (an API server before 1.30); or a second controller is granted `agentsandboxes/status`; or the datastore's operator stops being the cluster's administrator. Then build ADR-0057 decision 8 as designed |
| R-47 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0057).* A restore of the datastore from a backup puts back an earlier, authentic record under the same UID: it passes the binding check, and a claim released since the backup reads as live again, with the Secrets and the registration entry the same backup held. No check of authenticity would change this: it gives no freshness | A restore is the cluster administrator's act on the whole cluster, and the administrator is trusted (§9). What comes back is bounded by the record's own deadline (`ClaimRecord::is_live`), by the envelopes' expiry and the envelope and orphan sweeps at their next pass (TB-9), and by the issuer where it refuses a refresh token rotated since the backup; the pool member behind an ended claim has been recycled | Restores become routine, or are run by someone other than the cluster's administrator; or a broker-wide maximum claim TTL exists, from which a rule after a restore can be stated (release every claim older than the backup) |
| R-48 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0053 decisions 5 and 6).* The gateway's output chain is unproven on a VM and is not a default-deny. Its policy is `accept` and it names five users: root and every user it does not name keep every destination but the broker's address, and the authorizer's loopback check port stays open to them as before. It does not bound an internal address that is neither in the env file nor in the operator's `EGRESS_DENY_ELEMENTS`, a public address a name was not meant to reach, root (who can rewrite the table), or unix sockets; and a deployment that renders its own table has none of it. It is asserted in the rendered and the built image (`image/gateway/check.sh`) and has never been loaded by a booted gateway: neither the drops per user, nor that the resolver, the agent and the relay still work under it, have been observed | A default of `drop` needs the list of every system user on the image that opens a connection, which only a booted image gives, and a wrong guess is a gateway that boots and serves nothing. The chain is loaded in the same transaction as the rest of the table, and no table means no listener. The broker's refusal of the listeners' identity does not depend on it (TB-6), and the firewall on the egress addresses stays the allowlist that does not depend on the gateway (R-20) | The live run of ADR-0053 on gateway VMs: the drops observed for each user and the resolver question settled, then the default moved to `drop`; and the tunnel check moved to the unix socket by default, which removes the loopback exception (ADR-0053 open questions 4, 6 and 7) |
| R-49 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0053, found at implementation).* An authorizer the broker has stopped accepting keeps serving what it has cached. The broker's refusal of an identity is in the handshake, which a client cannot tell from an outage, so the authorizer reports `broker_unreachable` and a warm cache serves a claim's allowlist for up to the stale bound (300 seconds). A gateway whose entry was just replaced or withdrawn is therefore not cut off at once | What is served is an allowlist the broker gave while that authorizer was a peer, never past the claim's deadline, and for a claim with nothing cached nothing at all; a cached credential is bounded separately, at 60 seconds (TB-17); it is the window an outage has had since ADR-0028 (TB-13). Telling the two apart would need the broker to complete the handshake with an identity it no longer admits and refuse at the route, which is what ADR-0053 decision 3 removed | A gateway must be cut off faster than the stale bound (then a shorter `--stale-secs`, or a refusal the broker can give after the handshake); or per-gateway identities are decided, which makes withdrawing one gateway an operation of its own |
| R-50 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0053 decision 7).* While `accept_plain_gateway_identity` is `true`, the broker's handshake and its two gateway routes answer the listeners' ID as well as the authorizer's: the compartment between a gateway's processes then rests on the gateway's table alone (R-48), and on a gateway still running an image from before the chain, on the processes behaving, as before ADR-0053 | The switch exists for an environment where the gateways' entries are another team's change on another day, so that the fix is not delayed by an outage of the whole egress path. It is off by default, and while on the broker warns at start and at every ask under the plain ID with the peer, the route and the claim UID (TB-6) | The switch is set back to `false` in every deployment (no warning line for a full claim lifetime), and the key is removed from the configuration, where unknown keys already refuse the start; until then this row stands |
| R-51 | *New 2026-11-19 (found while turning off session resumption on the gateway's claim listeners).* Which certificate validator Envoy applies to a claim's client certificate is not verified. The two templates name no validator: the bundle arrives as the SDS validation context and a matcher requires a URI SAN under the claim prefix (`image/gateway/envoy/gateway.yaml.in`, `inject.yaml.in`). Whether the secret the agent serves carries Envoy's SPIFFE validator or its default one is the agent's SDS setting, and has not been read from a running Envoy. Under the default one, "one URI SAN per certificate" is a property of the issuing CA and not something Envoy checks, and Envoy reports the first SAN as the peer | The only CA in the bundle is the trust domain's, and SPIRE puts exactly one URI SAN in an SVID, so a certificate with a second one would have to be issued by that CA. Session resumption is off on both listeners, stateless and stateful (accepted by Envoy 1.40.2), so every connection is validated against the bundle as it is now. The comments in both templates say what is and is not established | A configuration dump from a live Envoy on a gateway shows which validator the secret carries; then name it in the templates, or record here which one runs and what it checks |
| R-52 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0053); narrowed the same day by phase 2.* Under `policy.audiences: enforce` an unscoped audience is usable only by a claim whose policy grants it, so what follows holds for `shadow` and for a claim outside a policy. An audience with no `namespaces` stays usable from every namespace (the unchanged form), and a scoped one with no top-level `rules` has no ceiling, so its grants are bounded only by themselves. Both are the operator's configuration, read at start, and the broker logs each unscoped audience by name when the namespace map is set. A configuration change is a restart, so a removed grant takes effect for a bound claim at its next mint or gateway ask, not at once. *Revisit when* phase 2 of ADR-0053 (the verified policy bundle as the source of the grants, written by the platform and read-only to tenants) lands, or when every audience of a deployment is scoped (then refuse the unscoped form unless it says so in words) | Accepted |
| R-53 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0053 phase 2).* Under `policy.audiences: enforce` the broker depends on notaio re-signing: a bundle lives its lifetime (15 minutes by default, a site value up to a day) and is refreshed at half, so a notaio outage longer than that, or a broker clock ahead of notaio's by more than the refresh margin, takes the audiences away from every claim under a policy (fail closed, `an_expired_or_missing_bundle_takes_the_audiences_away`), and a claim created before `enforce`, with no recorded policy, loses them at its next ask. The switch is the rollback (`shadow` restores phase 1 on the next ask), and the rollout drains policy-less claims first. The tenant-read-only posture of the policy namespaces (RBAC and an admission policy limiting writers to the GitOps identity) is a requirement on the deployment and notaio's manifests that nothing in this repository enforces. *Revisit when* a notaio outage is a recurring cause of lost audiences (then a bounded grace on an expired bundle, which ADR-0026 deliberately has none of), or when the policy namespaces' RBAC is shipped from this repository | Accepted |
| R-54 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0054).* The namespace is the unit of policy eligibility: whoever may place a sandbox in a namespace may name every policy that namespace draws from, and under `enforce` gets its direct egress and its placed audiences. Two tenants mapped to one policy namespace (`policy.sources`, A-29) share every policy in it, and a mapping mistake gives a tenant a namespace's policies it was not meant to have. Which groups may use a policy (notaio's `spec.subjects.groups`) is not checked, because the signed bundle does not carry it and the unsigned `SandboxPolicy` resource is not read | The default draws from a sandbox's own namespace only, so a shared source has to be written by the operator; the mapping is validated at start and each namespace's sources are logged there (`check_policy_sources`, `log_policy_sources`, `crates/mediatore/src/config.rs`); an operator who needs tenants apart keeps each tenant's policies in a namespace only that tenant draws from. A mistake cannot place a sandbox, reach a pool or change what a token is minted for | notaio's bundle carries `subjects.groups`: then the broker checks the caller's groups against it on both paths, in an ADR of its own |
| R-55 | *New 2026-11-19 (ADR-0054 decision 4, found at implementation).* Under `enforce`, a watched policy namespace whose first list never completes on the replica holding the Lease holds every sandbox that names a policy and whose namespace draws from it: no claim is created, and the only signal is the sandbox's `PolicyConformant` condition, `Unknown`/`BundlesNotRead`, whose message names the namespace not yet read (`hold_until_read`, `crates/mediatore-claims/src/sandbox.rs`; `BundleCache::unread_sources`, `crates/mediatore-claims/src/policy.rs`). A namespace listed in `policy.namespaces` without the broker's `Role` there (`deploy/policy-bundles`) is the plain case: the hold does not end, the sandbox is never `Failed`, and one unread source holds the answer even when another source of that namespace holds the name. It is a denial of service on the namespaces that draw from that source, and it is stricter than before ADR-0054, when an unread cache still created the sandbox, without a policy on its record. The front door does not refuse in this state either: it creates the sandbox and the reconciler holds it | Fail closed: the alternative is to answer "not found" for a policy that may exist, or to create the sandbox without the policy it named, which is what ADR-0054 decision 4 ends. Only the sandbox's own sources are waited for, never every watched namespace (`a_namespace_is_not_read_until_its_own_sources_listed_and_no_other_is_waited_for`), a source the reflector does not watch is skipped and not waited for, a sandbox that names no policy is not held, and `shadow` holds nothing. Who lists a namespace and binds the `Role` is the operator, in one overlay (`deploy/policy-bundles`) | A hold has a deadline after which the sandbox is `Failed` with a reason; or the broker reports at start, or as a metric, a watched namespace it cannot list; or a deployment sees a sandbox held this way outside a broker's start |
| R-56 | *New 2026-11-20 (ADR-0055).* Whoever can write a team's Secret chooses the credential the broker hands out for that team's audience, from the next read (within `credential_refresh_secs`); whoever can write the platform's KV path or Secret does the same for a platform audience. This is the design: teams own their credentials, and the broker places what the source holds. What bounds it: the credential still goes only to the definition's endpoints, within its rules, for claims the namespace may make; a team cannot point the broker at another namespace's Secret. | Teams' Secrets are written by their GitOps or by External Secrets from their own OpenBao. *Revisit when* a source can carry a signature the broker could check, or when a team audience may reach an endpoint shared with another tenant. |
| R-57 | *New 2026-11-20 (ADR-0055).* The audit line of a token or a placed credential names the claim, the audience and the subject, not the definition's namespace, kind and bundle version the ADR's security analysis lists; which definition served is reconstructed from the admission lines (`audience definition admitted`, with namespace, name, kind and version) by time. | The admission and withdrawal lines are complete and timestamped. *Revisit when* an incident needs the provenance on the issuance line itself, or before `bundles` is the default. |
| R-58 | *New 2026-11-20 (ADR-0055).* Under `shadow` the broker reads and holds every catalog audience's credential although the file serves: memory exposure (A-31) with no use, accepted so the RBAC and the OpenBao policy are proven before `bundles` serves from them. | The values print redacted and are never logged (the kind tier asserts neither appears in the broker's log). *Revisit when* `shadow` is run for longer than a migration. |
| R-59 | *New 2026-11-20 (ADR-0055).* Under `bundles` two checks derived from `audiences[]` are not made: ADR-0043's early refusal of a sandbox whose image is on a gateway-placed registry and which does not name that registry's audience (the pull fails later instead), and the private CA of an audience (`caRef` is refused at admission, `NotBuilt`). Neither widens a reach; the first moves a refusal later, the second refuses outright. | ADR-0060 moves registries into a grant. *Revisit when* ADR-0060 is implemented, or a team needs a private CA. |
| R-60 | *New 2026-11-20 (ADR-0055 decision 2, amended at implementation).* Two decisions in one request may read two catalog snapshots: a definition withdrawn between them is refused or skipped by the later one, never assumed present, but one request is not one consistent view. | Each snapshot is whole and verified, and a change can only make the later decision refuse or follow the newer definition. *Revisit when* a decision is found that two snapshots could combine into a reach neither grants. |
| R-61 | *New 2026-11-21 (ADR-0061).* Until `policy.follow` is `enforce`, and `policy.audiences` with it, a running claim whose inner layer (the ruleset, the proxy's rules) is wider than its policy's newer bundle keeps it for the rest of its lease, and a lease shortened after the claim does not reach it. Under `policy.audiences: shadow` the rendered leg finds nothing at all, because the file, not the bundle, decides the routes. | The outer enforcement points (the mint, the gateway's allowlist and inject routes) read the current bundle at every ask under `policy.audiences: enforce` (ADR-0053 decision 12), so what is stale is the defence in depth, not the boundary; the `WouldRefuse` condition shows every case. The knob and tool legs do not exist until the guest renders from the bundle. *Revisit when* `policy.audiences: enforce` is live: then `policy.follow: enforce` after a week of shadow. |
| R-62 | *New 2026-11-21 (ADR-0061 open question 1).* Under `enforce` a narrowing of rules or egress ends every running sandbox whose guest rendered wider, at its next request, rather than narrowing it in place: one policy change can stop many sandboxes at once. | Chosen over a re-render, which Landlock cannot do for a running process; the sandbox's owner claims again, tokens already minted run out, nothing is revoked, and `shadow` counts the cases before anything is refused. *Revisit when* the guest re-reads its policy (notaio phase 3, D4), which could narrow nftables and the proxy in place. |
| R-63 | *New 2026-11-22 (ADR-0063).* The `role: registry` marker lives on the broker's `audiences[]` only: notaio's `SandboxAudience` has no such field yet, so under `policy.audience_definitions.mode: bundles` with gateways no registry is granted and every image is refused. And a broker older than ADR-0063, given a bundle that does carry the field, would ignore it and serve the registry audience to the workload as an ordinary route | The refusal is fail closed (nothing is pulled around the gateway, nothing is granted by default); the older broker's reading needs a notaio release that carries the field, so the order of rollout is the control: the broker first, then the field in notaio, then bundles that use it | notaio carries `role` and its read-only lint, and the pin moves; then this row closes for the first half and the rollout order is written into the guide |
| R-64 | *New 2026-11-22 (ADR-0063).* Every image's layers now cross a gateway, and nothing limits the bandwidth one claim's pull may take there: several large pulls at once on one gateway, or one slow link, are measured nowhere yet (the spike was one pull on one kind node) | The bytes were already the claim's own and the gateway already carries the workload's traffic the same way (TB-13's "one claim exhausts the data plane" row); a stalled pull is bounded in the guest, and a tunnel at `TUNNEL_MAX_SECS` | Measured on a live pool with several pulls at once; or a gateway is seen saturated by pulls; then a per-claim or per-pull bound at the gateway |
| R-65 | *New 2026-11-22 (ADR-0063 decision 8).* Before any table can load, the Kairos initramfs runs its own stages: its `05_network` writes a DHCP catch-all and its comment says `systemd-networkd` may run there, so a member may send a DHCP broadcast on the segment through a packet socket no table sees, and the base is what decides. The datasource stage, which ran there too, is now limited to local devices (`image/oem/00_datasource.yaml`) | The segment is the operator's and has no DHCP server (banlieue ADR-0109); a broadcast reaches neighbours on the segment at layer 2, which the hypervisor bounds, and carries nothing of a claim | A pool member's boot shows whether `systemd-networkd` runs in the initramfs (`journalctl -b` before the switch-root, the conntrack and the segment's capture); if it does, an override of `05_network` in the image's `/system/oem` |
| R-66 | *New 2026-11-26 (ADR-0067).* A site may build the overlays `FROM` its own VM base (`VM_BASE`), which this repository neither builds nor signs: whatever that base carries (an agent, a CA, a package from its own mirror) runs as root on every member or gateway built on it, and the four packages the sandbox overlay installs come from the base's apt sources. Only the overlay's prerequisite step (no `sshd`, the tools it needs) and `image/check.sh` / `image/gateway/check.sh` stand between that base and a VM | The base is the site's operating system, which it already owns and reviews; forcing ours on it is what stopped a site from using mediatore at all. The checks are the contract, and the published images keep this repository's base | A site-built overlay is used here, or the checks gain a base-level assertion (a package allowlist, no unexpected enabled unit) |
| R-67 | *New 2026-11-26 (ADR-0068).* The Azure rules are proven as text, not on an Azure VM: that `systemd-networkd` renews by unicast from its own UDP socket as `systemd-network` (so the one rule keeps the lease), that the asset tag is readable before `network-pre.target`, and that the ready report passes the table are read from the client, the platform's documentation and banlieue's spike, not observed under the table. A wrong reading fails closed (no ready report, `OSProvisioningTimedOut`; a lease kept only by the broadcast rebind), never open: no rule admits more than root, that one user and those ports | The renders are pinned and `nft` accepts them in the built image; the failure modes are refusals the provisioning state shows | The first pool member and gateway boot on Azure under the tables: the drop counters of `mediatore-host imds`, `mediatore-host drop` and `mediatore-gateway out wireserver` read, a renewal seen in `networkctl status`, `provisioningState` `Succeeded`; then this row closes |

## 9. Assumptions and out of scope

- A management cluster whose administrators are trusted; since ADR-0046 one broker may place claims for several teams, but the tenant separation it enforces is on its own front door only: the declarative path is Kubernetes RBAC's, and a person with cluster access to `agentsandboxes` or Secrets is outside the map (R-35). A cluster admin is trusted (they can read
  the broker's SA token regardless — and, in the homelab collapse of ADR-0005,
  the SPIRE signing key too; see §8 R-7).
- The VM image is the one this repository builds and publishes (ADR-0023),
  pinned by digest in the `VMImage`: it carries `nftables`, `mount`, `umount`,
  `tpm2-tools` and `qemu-guest-agent`, and the deployment's cloud-config layer
  names the resolvers, the trust domain, the server and the bundle; Kairos
  behaves as its documentation says about `/usr/local`, `/etc` and
  `install.bind_mounts` (§8 R-15). The segment lets members reach the registries
  their images live on (ADR-0021), and the segment and the hypervisor enforce
  "no inbound" and bound guest root (ADR-0010 decisions 1 and 4), which this
  repo cannot test. The members have the memory for the images they are asked
  to run: the sandbox tmpfs is RAM, bounded by `--sandbox-size`. A pull
  credential the operator configures is scoped at the registry to reading the
  pool's images (ADR-0022): mediatore cannot narrow what a token the operator
  issued can do.
- The DMI chassis asset tag is the hypervisor's (ADR-0068): Azure sets
  `7783-7084-3265-9085-8269-3286-77` on every VM, and nothing in the guest below
  root can change it. A hypervisor elsewhere that sets the same tag only has root
  admitted to an address that answers nothing there; guest root is bounded by the
  segment and the hypervisor, not by the table (ADR-0020 decision 7).
- The gateway VM image is the one this repository builds and publishes
  (ADR-0028 decision 10), pinned by digest in the gateway `VMImage`; the
  platform team applies the gateway entries against the agent IDs the SPIRE
  server logs; the network team trunks the segment's VLAN on every host of the
  zone, admits the three gateways' egress addresses at the SPIRE server, the
  broker and the audiences' endpoints, and nothing from the sandbox range
  (ADR-0027's and ADR-0028's consequences, deploying guide step 7); Envoy
  1.40.2 behaves on a VM as the kind tier observed it (X25519MLKEM768
  negotiated, a node SVID refused by the SPIFFE validator before any filter).
- In an environment with gateways the operator runs the registry mirror on the sandbox
  segment, declares it on the broker and on the pool, and fills it with the images the
  requesters may name (ADR-0029); a public registry is unreachable there by construction,
  and nothing in this repository can route to one.
- The IdP is authoritative for authentication and group membership. A group value in the namespace map means what the connector emits for it: two connectors can emit the same bare name, and listing it admits both populations (ADR-0046, R-34). Who may edit the broker's ConfigMap, and so the map, is the operator's RBAC, and tenant namespaces, their pools and their `VirtualMachinePool` RBAC are the operator's and banlieue's to create; the broker cannot check at start that a mapped namespace exists, and a create into one that does not fails with a `500 internal` (`FrontDoorError::Api`, `crates/mediatore-api/src/lib.rs`), nothing parked.
- An upstream that takes a person's own credential decides what it accepts for which protocol: a git host
  may sign people in through LDAP and still refuse that password for Git over HTTPS (ADR-0041 decision 7);
  mediatore cannot tell a password from a token and does not try. The requester's OS keeps its credential
  store's items to the requester's account; this repository cannot test that.
- The SPIRE Controller Manager, agent and SPIFFE CSI driver come from the
  hardened chart and are part of the cluster's node TCB; mediatore adds no
  authority to them and takes none from them beyond its own SVID.
- banlieue's own threat model covers the VM lifecycle, image supply chain,
  hypervisor credentials and the guest-to-host channels its providers read; this
  document starts where a pool member booted from its installed disk, and
  ADR-0019 makes that member's `GuestReady` mean attested and registered as
  well.
- notaio's own threat model covers its signer (the OpenBao transit key it may
  only request signatures from, its T8.2), the review of its policy repository
  and the ceiling's second approval, and its writes to the policy namespaces;
  this document trusts a bundle exactly as far as the keys the operator listed
  (A-19) and starts where a signed envelope sits in a ConfigMap.
- Denial of service beyond per-claim rate limiting (planned, `5.6` in the
  runbook) is out of scope pre-1.0.
- The broker's reach over the store is its ServiceAccount's RBAC, which the
  operator grants (`deploy/base/rbac.yaml`, A-11, ADR-0034): `patch` on
  `agentsandboxes/status`, `update` on their finalizers, `create` and `delete`
  on `agentsandboxes`, Secrets in its own namespace, and the full verb set on
  `sandboxnodes` and `policyfloors`. A floor moves down only by that grant used
  outside the broker: the broker's one write path is `advance_policy_floor`.
  An ended sandbox is held by the finalizer for the retention window and then
  deleted, the audit line being what remains of it.
- The datacenter firewall's allowance for the gateways to reach an audience's host, the OpenBao
  transit key and policy, and the upstream's terms for a delegated session are the operator's
  (ADR-0040, §8 R-29); this document assumes the gateways cannot reach anything the network team
  did not allow.
